It's more of a constant, low-level worry and tension that just hangs over me all day long.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this constant worry and tension, and has it been consistent over time or have you noticed any periods where it worsened or improved?\n\nPatient: It’s been going on pretty steadily for a little over a year now, starting right around when my divorce was finalized and I took on a massive new project at work. The worry and tension have been consistent since then, without any real periods of relief or improvement.\n\nDoctor: Have you been able to meet the demands of your job and take care of your responsibilities as a parent despite your constant worry and tension, or have these symptoms significantly interfered with your ability to function in daily life?\n\nPatient: It has definitely interfered a lot. I've missed key project deadlines at work, and my CIO actually pulled me aside recently because I seemed so distracted and tense. On top of that, I've been snapping at my kids and bailing on friends, so it really feels like both my work performance and my relationships are slipping.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned that activities you used to enjoy no longer bring you pleasure, but can you tell me if you've experienced a significant change in your appetite or weight during this period of constant worry and tension?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really noticed any significant changes in my appetite or weight. that his worrying was starting to jeopardize both his job and his relationship with his kids.\n- Chief Complaint: “I’m constantly worrying about everything—my kids, my job, money—and it feels like it never lets up.”\n▸ Patient-specific Symptom Manifestations\n- S027 (Sleep_Disturbance): Most nights I lie in bed exhausted but my brain keeps replaying work presentations and arguments with my ex, so wake up, my mind starts running through worst-case scenarios about my kids hating the custody schedule, getting pushed out of this project, or not being able to retire, and that low-level panic just follows me all day no matter what I’m doing.\n- S057 (Uncontrollable_Worry): Even when I tell myself, 'There’s nothing you can do about this right now, just let it go,' the worries just morph into another angle—like if I stop thinking about the project for a second, I’ll miss something huge and everything will fall apart—so it feels impossible to switch it off.\n- S058 (Restlessness_Tension): I feel like I’ve had a motor running inside me for months: I’m always jiggling my leg in meetings, pacing around my apartment, clenching my jaw, and I can’t just sit and watch a game with my buddies without feeling like I should be doing something urgent.\n- S060 (Irritability): Lately the smallest things, like my son forgetting his backpack or a junior developer asking a basic question, make me snap or respond way sharper than I mean to, and then I feel guilty afterward because I know it’s really my nerves being fried, not them.\n▸ Additional Diagnostic Requirements\n- functional_impairment: That's paired with a deep physical exhaustion because my sleep is terrible—I just toss and turn all night with my mind racing.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced sudden, intense episodes of fear or discomfort that peak within minutes, which might be accompanied by symptoms like a racing heart, shortness of breath, or feeling like you're going to pass out?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't experienced sudden, intense panic attacks like that. It's more of a constant, low-level worry and tension that just hangs over me all day long.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this constant worry and tension, and has it been consistent over time or have you noticed any periods where it worsened or improved?\n\nPatient: It’s been going on pretty steadily for a little over a year now, starting right around when my divorce was finalized and I took on a massive new project at work. The worry and tension have been consistent since then, without any real periods of relief or improvement.\n\nDoctor: Have you been able to meet the demands of your job and take care of your responsibilities as a parent despite your constant worry and tension, or have these symptoms significantly interfered with your ability to function in daily life?\n\nPatient: It has definitely interfered a lot. I've missed key project deadlines at work, and my CIO actually pulled me aside recently because I seemed so distracted and tense. On top of that, I've been snapping at my kids and bailing on friends, so it really feels like both my work performance and my relationships are slipping.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned that activities you used to enjoy no longer bring you pleasure, but can you tell me if you've experienced a significant change in your appetite or weight during this period of constant worry and tension?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really noticed any significant changes in my appetite or weight. That hasn't been a main issue for me at all. My eating habits have stayed pretty much the same despite all the stress.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned snapping at your kids, can you tell me more about how your worry and tension have affected your mood and irritability towards others?\n\nPatient: My nerves are constantly fried, so I have a really short fuse and end up snapping over the smallest things. For instance, I'll get angry at my son for forgetting his backpack or respond way too sharply to a junior developer at work. I always feel incredibly guilty afterward because they don't deserve it, but I just feel too overwhelmed in the moment to control it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant fatigue or exhaustion after even minor mental or physical exertion, beyond what you'd expect from just being worried or stressed?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really noticed that specific kind of exhaustion after minor tasks. It's more of a general, heavy tiredness from tossing and turning all night and having my mind constantly running.\n\nDoctor: You've described a long-standing pattern of worry and tension that interferes with your daily life, but can you tell me if there are any specific situations or themes that your worries tend to focus on, such as health, finances, or relationships?"
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, my worries are heavily focused on a few main areas: my kids, my job, and my finances. I constantly stress over how my kids are handling our custody schedule after the divorce, whether I'm going to fail this massive digital transformation project and get pushed out of my company, and if I'll ever be financially stable enough to retire. These same themes just constantly cycle in my head all day long.

I just feel completely exhausted by it all and realized I need some help.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find pleasure in activities that you used to enjoy, or has your worry and exhaustion affected your ability to feel happy or interested in things?\n\nPatient: It's definitely taken the joy out of things; activities I used to love like hikes, live music, and soccer are now completely overshadowed by my mind constantly cycling through \"what if\" scenarios. I can't even watch a game with my buddies without feeling a restless urge that I should be doing something urgent, so I've just started bailing on plans entirely.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience any repetitive thoughts or mental images that you feel compelled to repeat, or any specific rituals or behaviors that you feel driven to perform in response to your worries?\n\nPatient: No, I don't have any compulsive rituals or repetitive mental images that I feel forced to perform. My mind just constantly runs through realistic worries and 'what-if' scenarios about my everyday life, like my job and my kids. It's just a non-stop loop of worrying about real-world things.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any physical symptoms like trembling, sweating, or difficulty breathing when your worries peak, or are your symptoms mostly limited to feeling mentally exhausted and preoccupied?\n\nPatient: No, I don't really experience trembling, sweating, or trouble breathing. Instead, I have this constant physical restlessness and tension, like an internal motor is running that makes me jiggle my leg, pace, and clench my jaw. That's paired with a deep physical exhaustion because my sleep is terrible—I just toss and turn all night with my mind racing.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced sudden, intense episodes of fear or discomfort that peak within minutes, which might be accompanied by symptoms like a racing heart, shortness of breath, or feeling like you're going to pass out?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't experienced sudden, intense panic attacks like that. It's more of a constant, low-level worry and tension that just hangs over me all day long.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this constant worry and tension, and has it been consistent over time or have you noticed any periods where it worsened or improved?\n\nPatient: It’s been going on pretty steadily for a little over a year now, starting right around when my divorce was finalized and I took on a massive new project at work. The worry and tension have been consistent since then, without any real periods of relief or improvement.\n\nDoctor: Have you been able to meet the demands of your job and take care of your responsibilities as a parent despite your constant worry and tension, or have these symptoms significantly interfered with your ability to function in daily life?\n\nPatient: It has definitely interfered a lot. I've missed key project deadlines at work, and my CIO actually pulled me aside recently because I seemed so distracted and tense. On top of that, I've been snapping at my kids and bailing on friends, so it really feels like both my work performance and my relationships are slipping.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned that activities you used to enjoy no longer bring you pleasure, but can you tell me if you've experienced a significant change in your appetite or weight during this period of constant worry and tension?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really noticed any significant changes in my appetite or weight. That hasn't been a main issue for me at all. My eating habits have stayed pretty much the same despite all the stress.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned snapping at your kids, can you tell me more about how your worry and tension have affected your mood and irritability towards others?\n\nPatient: My nerves are constantly fried, so I have a really short fuse and end up snapping over the smallest things. For instance, I'll get angry at my son for forgetting his backpack or respond way too sharply to a junior developer at work. I always feel incredibly guilty afterward because they don't deserve it, but I just feel too overwhelmed in the moment to control it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant fatigue or exhaustion after even minor mental or physical exertion, beyond what you'd expect from just being worried or stressed?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really noticed that specific kind of exhaustion after minor tasks. It's more of a general, heavy tiredness from tossing and turning all night and having my mind constantly running.\n\nDoctor: You've described a long-standing pattern of worry and tension that interferes with your daily life, but can you tell me if there are any specific situations or themes that your worries tend to focus on, such as health, finances, or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes, my worries are heavily focused on a few main areas: my kids, my job, and my finances. I constantly stress over how my kids are handling our custody schedule after the divorce, whether I'm going to fail this massive digital transformation project and get pushed out of my company, and if I'll ever be financially stable enough to retire. These same themes just constantly cycle in my head all day long.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned that activities you used to enjoy no longer bring you pleasure, but are there any activities or things that you still look forward to or that can temporarily distract you from your worries?\n\nPatient: Honestly, nothing really succeeds in distracting me right now. Even when I try to do things I used to enjoy, like hiking, going to live music, or watching a game with my friends, my mind just keeps cycling through those \"what-if\" scenarios. I just feel too restless to actually relax or switch off the worry, so it never really stops.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt so down or hopeless about your situation that you've thought you'd be better off dead, or had any thoughts of self-harm?\n\nPatient: No, I have never had any thoughts of wanting to end my life or hurting myself. Even though I feel incredibly stressed, overwhelmed, and exhausted by everything going on, I've never felt that way."

Can you please tell me what brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m constantly worrying about everything—my kids, my job, money—and it feels like it never lets up. I just feel completely exhausted by it all and realized I need some help.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find pleasure in activities that you used to enjoy, or has your worry and exhaustion affected your ability to feel happy or interested in things?\n\nPatient: It's definitely taken the joy out of things; activities I used to love like hikes, live music, and soccer are now completely overshadowed by my mind constantly cycling through \"what if\" scenarios. I can't even watch a game with my buddies without feeling a restless urge that I should be doing something urgent, so I've just started bailing on plans entirely.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience any repetitive thoughts or mental images that you feel compelled to repeat, or any specific rituals or behaviors that you feel driven to perform in response to your worries?\n\nPatient: No, I don't have any compulsive rituals or repetitive mental images that I feel forced to perform. My mind just constantly runs through realistic worries and 'what-if' scenarios about my everyday life, like my job and my kids. It's just a non-stop loop of worrying about real-world things.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any physical symptoms like trembling, sweating, or difficulty breathing when your worries peak, or are your symptoms mostly limited to feeling mentally exhausted and preoccupied?\n\nPatient: No, I don't really experience trembling, sweating, or trouble breathing. Instead, I have this constant physical restlessness and tension, like an internal motor is running that makes me jiggle my leg, pace, and clench my jaw. That's paired with a deep physical exhaustion because my sleep is terrible—I just toss and turn all night with my mind racing.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced sudden, intense episodes of fear or discomfort that peak within minutes, which might be accompanied by symptoms like a racing heart, shortness of breath, or feeling like you're going to pass out?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't experienced sudden, intense panic attacks like that. It's more of a constant, low-level worry and tension that just hangs over me all day long.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this constant worry and tension, and has it been consistent over time or have you noticed any periods where it worsened or improved?\n\nPatient: It’s been going on pretty steadily for a little over a year now, starting right around when my divorce was finalized and I took on a massive new project at work. The worry and tension have been consistent since then, without any real periods of relief or improvement.\n\nDoctor: Have you been able to meet the demands of your job and take care of your responsibilities as a parent despite your constant worry and tension, or have these symptoms significantly interfered with your ability to function in daily life?\n\nPatient: It has definitely interfered a lot. I've missed key project deadlines at work, and my CIO actually pulled me aside recently because I seemed so distracted and tense. On top of that, I've been snapping at my kids and bailing on friends, so it really feels like both my work performance and my relationships are slipping.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned that activities you used to enjoy no longer bring you pleasure, but can you tell me if you've experienced a significant change in your appetite or weight during this period of constant worry and tension?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really noticed any significant changes in my appetite or weight. That hasn't been a main issue for me at all. My eating habits have stayed pretty much the same despite all the stress.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned snapping at your kids, can you tell me more about how your worry and tension have affected your mood and irritability towards others?\n\nPatient: My nerves are constantly fried, so I have a really short fuse and end up snapping over the smallest things. For instance, I'll get angry at my son for forgetting his backpack or respond way too sharply to a junior developer at work. I always feel incredibly guilty afterward because they don't deserve it, but I just feel too overwhelmed in the moment to control it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant fatigue or exhaustion after even minor mental or physical exertion, beyond what you'd expect from just being worried or stressed?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really noticed that specific kind of exhaustion after minor tasks. It's more of a general, heavy tiredness from tossing and turning all night and having my mind constantly running.\n\nDoctor: You've described a long-standing pattern of worry and tension that interferes with your daily life, but can you tell me if there are any specific situations or themes that your worries tend to focus on, such as health, finances, or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes, my worries are heavily focused on a few main areas: my kids, my job, and my finances. I constantly stress over how my kids are handling our custody schedule after the divorce, whether I'm going to fail this massive digital transformation project and get pushed out of my company, and if I'll ever be financially stable enough to retire. These same themes just constantly cycle in my head all day long.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned that activities you used to enjoy no longer bring you pleasure, but are there any activities or things that you still look forward to or that can temporarily distract you from your worries?\n\nPatient: Honestly, nothing really succeeds in distracting me right now. Even when I try to do things I used to enjoy, like hiking, going to live music, or watching a game with my friends, my mind just keeps cycling through those \"what-if\" scenarios. I just feel too restless to actually relax or switch off the worry, so it never really stops.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt so down or hopeless about your situation that you've thought you'd be better off dead, or had any thoughts of self-harm?\n\nPatient: No, I have never had any thoughts of wanting to end my life or hurting myself. Even though I feel incredibly stressed, overwhelmed, and exhausted by everything going on, I've never felt that way.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe a typical day and how your worry and tension affect your daily routine, including any times when you might feel a bit better or worse?\n\nPatient: From the moment I wake up, a low-level panic follows me all day, and the physical tension—like clenching my jaw and jiggling my leg—makes it hard to get through work meetings. It gets even worse at night when I'm trying to sleep, as my mind just races with worries while I toss and turn for hours. There really aren't any times when I feel better, as the worry is pretty much constant from morning to night."

    "content": "Previous Candidates:\n[\"F41.1\", \"F32.0\"]\n\nInterview Transcript:\n\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you please tell me what brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m constantly worrying about everything—my kids, my job, money—and it feels like it never lets up. I just feel completely exhausted by it all and realized I need some help.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find pleasure in activities that you used to enjoy, or has your worry and exhaustion affected your ability to feel happy or interested in things?\n\nPatient: It's definitely taken the joy out of things; activities I used to love like hikes, live music, and soccer are now completely overshadowed by my mind constantly cycling through \"what if\" scenarios. I can't even watch a game with my buddies without feeling a restless urge that I should be doing something urgent, so I've just started bailing on plans entirely.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience any repetitive thoughts or mental images that you feel compelled to repeat, or any specific rituals or behaviors that you feel driven to perform in response to your worries?\n\nPatient: No, I don't have any compulsive rituals or repetitive mental images that I feel forced to perform. My mind just constantly runs through realistic worries and 'what-if' scenarios about my everyday life, like my job and my kids. It's just a non-stop loop of worrying about real-world things.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any physical symptoms like trembling, sweating, or difficulty breathing when your worries peak, or are your symptoms mostly limited to feeling mentally exhausted and preoccupied?\n\nPatient: No, I don't really experience trembling, sweating, or trouble breathing. Instead, I have this constant physical restlessness and tension, like an internal motor is running that makes me jiggle my leg, pace, and clench my jaw. That's paired with a deep physical exhaustion because my sleep is terrible—I just toss and turn all night with my mind racing.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced sudden, intense episodes of fear or discomfort that peak within minutes, which might be accompanied by symptoms like a racing heart, shortness of breath, or feeling like you're going to pass out?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't experienced sudden, intense panic attacks like that. It's more of a constant, low-level worry and tension that just hangs over me all day long.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this constant worry and tension, and has it been consistent over time or have you noticed any periods where it worsened or improved?\n\nPatient: It’s been going on pretty steadily for a little over a year now, starting right around when my divorce was finalized and I took on a massive new project at work. The worry and tension have been consistent since then, without any real periods of relief or improvement.\n\nDoctor: Have you been able to meet the demands of your job and take care of your responsibilities as a parent despite your constant worry and tension, or have these symptoms significantly interfered with your ability to function in daily life?\n\nPatient: It has definitely interfered a lot. I've missed key project deadlines at work, and my CIO actually pulled me aside recently because I seemed so distracted and tense. On top of that, I've been snapping at my kids and bailing on friends, so it really feels like both my work performance and my relationships are slipping.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned that activities you used to enjoy no longer bring you pleasure, but can you tell me if you've experienced a significant change in your appetite or weight during this period of constant worry and tension?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really noticed any significant changes in my appetite or weight. That hasn't been a main issue for me at all. My eating habits have stayed pretty much the same despite all the stress.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned snapping at your kids, can you tell me more about how your worry and tension have affected your mood and irritability towards others?\n\nPatient: My nerves are constantly fried, so I have a really short fuse and end up snapping over the smallest things. For instance, I'll get angry at my son for forgetting his backpack or respond way too sharply to a junior developer at work. I always feel incredibly guilty afterward because they don't deserve it, but I just feel too overwhelmed in the moment to control it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant fatigue or exhaustion after even minor mental or physical exertion, beyond what you'd expect from just being worried or stressed?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really noticed that specific kind of exhaustion after minor tasks. It's more of a general, heavy tiredness from tossing and turning all night and having my mind constantly running.\n\nDoctor: You've described a long-standing pattern of worry and tension that interferes with your daily life, but can you tell me if there are any specific situations or themes that your worries tend to focus on, such as health, finances, or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes, my worries are heavily focused on a few main areas: my kids, my job, and my finances. I constantly stress over how my kids are handling our custody schedule after the divorce, whether I'm going to fail this massive digital transformation project and get pushed out of my company, and if I'll ever be financially stable enough to retire. These same themes just constantly cycle in my head all day long.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned that activities you used to enjoy no longer bring you pleasure, but are there any activities or things that you still look forward to or that can temporarily distract you from your worries?\n\nPatient: Honestly, nothing really succeeds in distracting me right now. Even when I try to do things I used to enjoy, like hiking, going to live music, or watching a game with my friends, my mind just keeps cycling through those \"what-if\" scenarios. I just feel too restless to actually relax or switch off the worry, so it never really stops.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt so down or hopeless about your situation that you've thought you'd be better off dead, or had any thoughts of self-harm?\n\nPatient: No, I have never had any thoughts of wanting to end my life or hurting myself. Even though I feel incredibly stressed, overwhelmed, and exhausted by everything going on, I've never felt that way.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe a typical day and how your worry and tension affect your daily routine, including any times when you might feel a bit better or worse?\n\nPatient: From the moment I wake up, a low-level panic follows me all day, and the physical tension—like clenching my jaw and jiggling my leg—makes it hard to get through work meetings. It gets even worse at night when I'm trying to sleep, as my mind just races with worries while I toss and turn for hours. There really aren't any times when I feel better, as the worry is pretty much constant from morning to night.\n\nDoctor: You've described a constant state of worry and tension, but can you tell me if you've experienced a persistent feeling of sadness or emptiness that lasts for most of the day, or is it more of an anxious, on-edge feeling that dominates your experience?\n\nPatient: It is definitely the anxious, on-edge feeling that dominates my life rather than sadness or emptiness. I feel like I constantly have a motor running inside me, with my mind racing and a low-level panic following me around all day. I don't feel depressed or empty, just incredibly wound up and completely unable to turn my brain off."
  }

The patient's persistent anxiety and worry, despite the absence of depressive symptoms like sadness or emptiness, further supports this diagnosis. Therefore, F41.1 remains as the primary candidate, with the other option being less likely based on the information provided.", "is_final": false}

Even when I try to do things I used to enjoy, like hiking, going to live music, or watching a game with my friends, my mind just keeps cycling through those \"what-if\" scenarios. I just feel too restless to actually relax or switch off the worry, so it never really stops.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt so down or hopeless about your situation that you've thought you'd be better off dead, or had any thoughts of self-harm?\n\nPatient: No, I have never had any thoughts of wanting to end my life or hurting myself. Even though I feel incredibly stressed, overwhelmed, and exhausted by everything going on, I've never felt that way.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe a typical day and how your worry and tension affect your daily routine, including any times when you might feel a bit better or worse?\n\nPatient: From the moment I wake up, a low-level panic follows me all day, and the physical tension—like clenching my jaw and jiggling my leg—makes it hard to get through work meetings. It gets even worse at night when I'm trying to sleep, as my mind just races with worries while I toss and turn for hours. There really aren't any times when I feel better, as the worry is pretty much constant from morning to night.\n\nDoctor: You've described a constant state of worry and tension, but can you tell me if you've experienced a persistent feeling of sadness or emptiness that lasts for most of the day, or is it more of an anxious, on-edge feeling that dominates your experience?\n\nPatient: It is definitely the anxious, on-edge feeling that dominates my life rather than sadness or emptiness. I feel like I constantly have a motor running inside me, with my mind racing and a low-level panic following me around all day. I don't feel depressed or empty, just incredibly wound up and completely unable to turn my brain off.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant avoidance behaviors, where you deliberately avoid certain situations, places, or activities because they trigger or exacerbate your worry and tension?"
  }

Yes, I have. I've been bailing on social plans with my friends, like hikes or live music nights, because my mind is just too busy worrying for me to actually enjoy them. I’ve also started procrastinating on important work tasks because the pressure and constant worry make it feel too overwhelming to even face them.

I can't even watch a game with my buddies without feeling a restless urge that I should be doing something urgent, so I've just started bailing on plans entirely.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience any repetitive thoughts or mental images that you feel compelled to repeat, or any specific rituals or behaviors that you feel driven to perform in response to your worries?\n\nPatient: No, I don't have any compulsive rituals or repetitive mental images that I feel forced to perform. My mind just constantly runs through realistic worries and 'what-if' scenarios about my everyday life, like my job and my kids. It's just a non-stop loop of worrying about real-world things.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any physical symptoms like trembling, sweating, or difficulty breathing when your worries peak, or are your symptoms mostly limited to feeling mentally exhausted and preoccupied?\n\nPatient: No, I don't really experience trembling, sweating, or trouble breathing. Instead, I have this constant physical restlessness and tension, like an internal motor is running that makes me jiggle my leg, pace, and clench my jaw. That's paired with a deep physical exhaustion because my sleep is terrible—I just toss and turn all night with my mind racing.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced sudden, intense episodes of fear or discomfort that peak within minutes, which might be accompanied by symptoms like a racing heart, shortness of breath, or feeling like you're going to pass out?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't experienced sudden, intense panic attacks like that. It's more of a constant, low-level worry and tension that just hangs over me all day long.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this constant worry and tension, and has it been consistent over time or have you noticed any periods where it worsened or improved?\n\nPatient: It’s been going on pretty steadily for a little over a year now, starting right around when my divorce was finalized and I took on a massive new project at work. The worry and tension have been consistent since then, without any real periods of relief or improvement.\n\nDoctor: Have you been able to meet the demands of your job and take care of your responsibilities as a parent despite your constant worry and tension, or have these symptoms significantly interfered with your ability to function in daily life?\n\nPatient: It has definitely interfered a lot. I've missed key project deadlines at work, and my CIO actually pulled me aside recently because I seemed so distracted and tense. On top of that, I've been snapping at my kids and bailing on friends, so it really feels like both my work performance and my relationships are slipping.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned that activities you used to enjoy no longer bring you pleasure, but can you tell me if you've experienced a significant change in your appetite or weight during this period of constant worry and tension?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really noticed any significant changes in my appetite or weight. That hasn't been a main issue for me at all. My eating habits have stayed pretty much the same despite all the stress.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned snapping at your kids, can you tell me more about how your worry and tension have affected your mood and irritability towards others?\n\nPatient: My nerves are constantly fried, so I have a really short fuse and end up snapping over the smallest things. For instance, I'll get angry at my son for forgetting his backpack or respond way too sharply to a junior developer at work. I always feel incredibly guilty afterward because they don't deserve it, but I just feel too overwhelmed in the moment to control it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant fatigue or exhaustion after even minor mental or physical exertion, beyond what you'd expect from just being worried or stressed?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really noticed that specific kind of exhaustion after minor tasks. It's more of a general, heavy tiredness from tossing and turning all night and having my mind constantly running.\n\nDoctor: You've described a long-standing pattern of worry and tension that interferes with your daily life, but can you tell me if there are any specific situations or themes that your worries tend to focus on, such as health, finances, or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes, my worries are heavily focused on a few main areas: my kids, my job, and my finances. I constantly stress over how my kids are handling our custody schedule after the divorce, whether I'm going to fail this massive digital transformation project and get pushed out of my company, and if I'll ever be financially stable enough to retire. These same themes just constantly cycle in my head all day long.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned that activities you used to enjoy no longer bring you pleasure, but are there any activities or things that you still look forward to or that can temporarily distract you from your worries?\n\nPatient: Honestly, nothing really succeeds in distracting me right now. Even when I try to do things I used to enjoy, like hiking, going to live music, or watching a game with my friends, my mind just keeps cycling through those \"what-if\" scenarios. I just feel too restless to actually relax or switch off the worry, so it never really stops.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt so down or hopeless about your situation that you've thought you'd be better off dead, or had any thoughts of self-harm?\n\nPatient: No, I have never had any thoughts of wanting to end my life or hurting myself. Even though I feel incredibly stressed, overwhelmed, and exhausted by everything going on, I've never felt that way.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe a typical day and how your worry and tension affect your daily routine, including any times when you might feel a bit better or worse?\n\nPatient: From the moment I wake up, a low-level panic follows me all day, and the physical tension—like clenching my jaw and jiggling my leg—makes it hard to get through work meetings. It gets even worse at night when I'm trying to sleep, as my mind just races with worries while I toss and turn for hours. There really aren't any times when I feel better, as the worry is pretty much constant from morning to night.\n\nDoctor: You've described a constant state of worry and tension, but can you tell me if you've experienced a persistent feeling of sadness or emptiness that lasts for most of the day, or is it more of an anxious, on-edge feeling that dominates your experience?\n\nPatient: It is definitely the anxious, on-edge feeling that dominates my life rather than sadness or emptiness. I feel like I constantly have a motor running inside me, with my mind racing and a low-level panic following me around all day. I don't feel depressed or empty, just incredibly wound up and completely unable to turn my brain off.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant avoidance behaviors, where you deliberately avoid certain situations, places, or activities because they trigger or exacerbate your worry and tension?\n\nPatient: Yes, I have. I've been bailing on social plans with my friends, like hikes or live music nights, because my mind is just too busy worrying for me to actually enjoy them. I’ve also started procrastinating on important work tasks because the pressure and constant worry make it feel too overwhelming to even face them.\n\nDoctor: You've mentioned feeling anxious and on-edge most of the time, but can you tell me if there are any moments or situations where you feel a significant decrease in your anxiety, even if it's brief, or does the anxiety remain relatively constant?"
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
The anxiety is pretty much constant and feels completely impossible to switch off. Even when I try to do things I used to enjoy to relax—like going for a hike or out for live music—the worries about my work, finances, and kids still hover in the background. It just keeps me in a state of low-level panic all day long, no matter what I'm doing.

He decided to seek psychiatric help after missing important project deadlines and having his CIO pull him aside to express concern that he seemed unusually tense and preoccupied, which made him fear Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you please tell me what brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m constantly worrying about everything—my kids, my job, money—and it feels like it never lets up. I just feel completely exhausted by it all and realized I need some help.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find pleasure in activities that you used to enjoy, or has your worry and exhaustion affected your ability to feel happy or interested in things?\n\nPatient: It's definitely taken the joy out of things; activities I used to love like hikes, live music, and soccer are now completely overshadowed by my mind constantly cycling through \"what if\" scenarios. I can't even watch a game with my buddies without feeling a restless urge that I should be doing something urgent, so I've just started bailing on plans entirely.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience any repetitive thoughts or mental images that you feel compelled to repeat, or any specific rituals or behaviors that you feel driven to perform in response to your worries?\n\nPatient: No, I don't have any compulsive rituals or repetitive mental images that I feel forced to perform. My mind just constantly runs through realistic worries and 'what-if' scenarios about my everyday life, like my job and my kids. It's just a non-stop loop of worrying about real-world things.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any physical symptoms like trembling, sweating, or difficulty breathing when your worries peak, or are your symptoms mostly limited to feeling mentally exhausted and preoccupied?\n\nPatient: No, I don't really experience trembling, sweating, or trouble breathing. Instead, I have this constant physical restlessness and tension, like an internal motor is running that makes me jiggle my leg, pace, and clench my jaw. That's paired with a deep physical exhaustion because my sleep is terrible—I just toss and turn all night with my mind racing.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced sudden, intense episodes of fear or discomfort that peak within minutes, which might be accompanied by symptoms like a racing heart, shortness of breath, or feeling like you're going to pass out?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't experienced sudden, intense panic attacks like that. It's more of a constant, low-level worry and tension that just hangs over me all day long.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this constant worry and tension, and has it been consistent over time or have you noticed any periods where it worsened or improved?\n\nPatient: It’s been going on pretty steadily for a little over a year now, starting right around when my divorce was finalized and I took on a massive new project at work. The worry and tension have been consistent since then, without any real periods of relief or improvement.\n\nDoctor: Have you been able to meet the demands of your job and take care of your responsibilities as a parent despite your constant worry and tension, or have these symptoms significantly interfered with your ability to function in daily life?\n\nPatient: It has definitely interfered a lot. I've missed key project deadlines at work, and my CIO actually pulled me aside recently because I seemed so distracted and tense. On top of that, I've been snapping at my kids and bailing on friends, so it really feels like both my work performance and my relationships are slipping.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned that activities you used to enjoy no longer bring you pleasure, but can you tell me if you've experienced a significant change in your appetite or weight during this period of constant worry and tension?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really noticed any significant changes in my appetite or weight. That hasn't been a main issue for me at all. My eating habits have stayed pretty much the same despite all the stress.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned snapping at your kids, can you tell me more about how your worry and tension have affected your mood and irritability towards others?\n\nPatient: My nerves are constantly fried, so I have a really short fuse and end up snapping over the smallest things. For instance, I'll get angry at my son for forgetting his backpack or respond way too sharply to a junior developer at work. I always feel incredibly guilty afterward because they don't deserve it, but I just feel too overwhelmed in the moment to control it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant fatigue or exhaustion after even minor mental or physical exertion, beyond what you'd expect from just being worried or stressed?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really noticed that specific kind of exhaustion after minor tasks. It's more of a general, heavy tiredness from tossing and turning all night and having my mind constantly running.\n\nDoctor: You've described a long-standing pattern of worry and tension that interferes with your daily life, but can you tell me if there are any specific situations or themes that your worries tend to focus on, such as health, finances, or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes, my worries are heavily focused on a few main areas: my kids, my job, and my finances. I constantly stress over how my kids are handling our custody schedule after the divorce, whether I'm going to fail this massive digital transformation project and get pushed out of my company, and if I'll ever be financially stable enough to retire. These same themes just constantly cycle in my head all day long.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned that activities you used to enjoy no longer bring you pleasure, but are there any activities or things that you still look forward to or that can temporarily distract you from your worries?\n\nPatient: Honestly, nothing really succeeds in distracting me right now. Even when I try to do things I used to enjoy, like hiking, going to live music, or watching a game with my friends, my mind just keeps cycling through those \"what-if\" scenarios. I just feel too restless to actually relax or switch off the worry, so it never really stops.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt so down or hopeless about your situation that you've thought you'd be better off dead, or had any thoughts of self-harm?\n\nPatient: No, I have never had any thoughts of wanting to end my life or hurting myself. Even though I feel incredibly stressed, overwhelmed, and exhausted by everything going on, I've never felt that way.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe a typical day and how your worry and tension affect your daily routine, including any times when you might feel a bit better or worse?\n\nPatient: From the moment I wake up, a low-level panic follows me all day, and the physical tension—like clenching my jaw and jiggling my leg—makes it hard to get through work meetings. It gets even worse at night when I'm trying to sleep, as my mind just races with worries while I toss and turn for hours. There really aren't any times when I feel better, as the worry is pretty much constant from morning to night.\n\nDoctor: You've described a constant state of worry and tension, but can you tell me if you've experienced a persistent feeling of sadness or emptiness that lasts for most of the day, or is it more of an anxious, on-edge feeling that dominates your experience?\n\nPatient: It is definitely the anxious, on-edge feeling that dominates my life rather than sadness or emptiness. I feel like I constantly have a motor running inside me, with my mind racing and a low-level panic following me around all day. I don't feel depressed or empty, just incredibly wound up and completely unable to turn my brain off.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant avoidance behaviors, where you deliberately avoid certain situations, places, or activities because they trigger or exacerbate your worry and tension?\n\nPatient: Yes, I have. I've been bailing on social plans with my friends, like hikes or live music nights, because my mind is just too busy worrying for me to actually enjoy them. I’ve also started procrastinating on important work tasks because the pressure and constant worry make it feel too overwhelming to even face them.\n\nDoctor: You've mentioned feeling anxious and on-edge most of the time, but can you tell me if there are any moments or situations where you feel a significant decrease in your anxiety, even if it's brief, or does the anxiety remain relatively constant?\n\nPatient: The anxiety is pretty much constant and feels completely impossible to switch off. Even when I try to do things I used to enjoy to relax—like going for a hike or out for live music—the worries about my work, finances, and kids still hover in the background. It just keeps me in a state of low-level panic all day long, no matter what I'm doing.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how your constant worry and tension relate to the significant life changes you mentioned, such as your divorce and new work project, and whether you feel like your symptoms would improve if those stressors were resolved?"

Even if I try to let go of one stressor, my mind just finds a new angle to worry about, making it feel impossible to switch off.

I just feel too restless to actually relax or switch off the worry, so it never really stops.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt so down or hopeless about your situation that you've thought you'd be better off dead, or had any thoughts of self-harm?\n\nPatient: No, I have never had any thoughts of wanting to end my life or hurting myself. Even though I feel incredibly stressed, overwhelmed, and exhausted by everything going on, I've never felt that way.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe a typical day and how your worry and tension affect your daily routine, including any times when you might feel a bit better or worse?\n\nPatient: From the moment I wake up, a low-level panic follows me all day, and the physical tension—like clenching my jaw and jiggling my leg—makes it hard to get through work meetings. It gets even worse at night when I'm trying to sleep, as my mind just races with worries while I toss and turn for hours. There really aren't any times when I feel better, as the worry is pretty much constant from morning to night.\n\nDoctor: You've described a constant state of worry and tension, but can you tell me if you've experienced a persistent feeling of sadness or emptiness that lasts for most of the day, or is it more of an anxious, on-edge feeling that dominates your experience?\n\nPatient: It is definitely the anxious, on-edge feeling that dominates my life rather than sadness or emptiness. I feel like I constantly have a motor running inside me, with my mind racing and a low-level panic following me around all day. I don't feel depressed or empty, just incredibly wound up and completely unable to turn my brain off.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant avoidance behaviors, where you deliberately avoid certain situations, places, or activities because they trigger or exacerbate your worry and tension?\n\nPatient: Yes, I have. I've been bailing on social plans with my friends, like hikes or live music nights, because my mind is just too busy worrying for me to actually enjoy them. I’ve also started procrastinating on important work tasks because the pressure and constant worry make it feel too overwhelming to even face them.\n\nDoctor: You've mentioned feeling anxious and on-edge most of the time, but can you tell me if there are any moments or situations where you feel a significant decrease in your anxiety, even if it's brief, or does the anxiety remain relatively constant?\n\nPatient: The anxiety is pretty much constant and feels completely impossible to switch off. Even when I try to do things I used to enjoy to relax—like going for a hike or out for live music—the worries about my work, finances, and kids still hover in the background. It just keeps me in a state of low-level panic all day long, no matter what I'm doing.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how your constant worry and tension relate to the significant life changes you mentioned, such as your divorce and new work project, and whether you feel like your symptoms would improve if those stressors were resolved?\n\nPatient: This all started a little over a year ago when my divorce was finalized and I took on that massive project, with my worries directly tied to those events like custody schedules and missing deadlines. While I wish resolving them would fix everything, it feels like this anxiety has taken on a life of its own now. Even if I try to let go of one stressor, my mind just finds a new angle to worry about, making it feel impossible to switch off."

Subtypes: Childlike Silliness, Stupor, Catalepsy, Waxy Flexibility, Mutism, Negativism, Posturing, Mannerism, Stereotypy, Agitation, Grimacing, Echolalia, Echopraxia\n[S023] Negative_Symptoms: A state where emotions, motivation, speech, or social interest that should normally be present are diminished or lost.  Subtypes: Diminished Emotional Expression, Avolition, Alogia, Anhedonia, Asociality\n[S024] Depressed_Mood: A state where a sad, empty, or hopeless mood persists.  Subtypes: Depressed Mood, Tearfulness, Sadness, Emptiness, Emotional Numbness\n[S025] Loss_of_Interest: A state where interest or pleasure in previously enjoyed activities is markedly diminished.  Subtypes: Loss of Interest, Social Withdrawal, Loss of Motivation, Anhedonia, Indifference to Achievement\n[S026] Weight_Appetite_Change: Significant increase or decrease in appetite or weight.  Subtypes: Significant Weight Loss, Weight Gain, Decrease in Appetite, Increase in Appetite\n[S027] Sleep_Disturbance: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.  Subtypes: Initial Insomnia, Middle Insomnia, Early Morning Awakening, Hypersomnia, Nightmare Disorder, Non-Restorative Sleep\n[S028] Psychomotor_Changes: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.  Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.  Subtypes: Chronic Fatigue, Physical Fatigue, Mental Fatigue, Loss of Energy\n[S030] Worthlessness_Guilt: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Ruminative Guilt, Excessive or Inappropriate Guilt, Delusional Guilt\n[S031] Diminished_Thinking: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.  Subtypes: Reduced Concentration, Indecisiveness, Subjective Memory Complaints, Clouded Thinking\n[S032] Death_Suicide: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt. Subtypes: Racing Thoughts, Flooding of Thoughts, Inability to Control Thoughts\n[S039] Manic_Distractibility: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli. Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences. Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world. Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities. Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.
